# Frontispiece

<insert>Rv <note>Likely an abbreviation for "Recto," indicating the front side of the leaf.</note></insert>

<image-desc>An oval emblem or bookplate is centered on a white rectangular paper. The emblem features a blue background with a **pelican in her piety** <term>pelican in her piety: a traditional symbol of a mother pelican wounding her own breast to feed her young with her blood; in alchemy, it represents the stage of "multiplication" or spiritual revitalization</term> perched upon a nest. Below the nest is a square shield or pedestal containing a **cross with four roses** in the quadrants <note>the "Rose Cross," the primary symbol of the Rosicrucian brotherhood, representing the union of the spirit (cross) and the soul or unfolding life (roses)</note>. Rays of light descend from a golden bowl or sun at the top, representing divine illumination. Surrounding the central image is a circular inscription in pale gold lettering.</image-desc>

->**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**<-
<note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA." Hermeticism is a philosophical and esoteric tradition based on writings attributed to the legendary sage Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

***

**Terminology and Symbolism:**

*   **Hermetic Philosophy**: A tradition of ancient wisdom that seeks to understand the laws of nature and the universe through alchemy, astrology, and spiritual practice.
*   **The Pelican**: Beyond its Christian associations with sacrifice, alchemists used the pelican to symbolize the "feeding" of the "philosopher's stone" with the essence of the work.
*   **The Rose Cross**: This emblem identifies the volume as part of the Rosicrucian tradition, a secret society of "Christian kabbalists" and "alchemical philosophers" that rose to prominence in the early 17th century.
*   **Marbled Paper**: The background of this leaf matches the "snail" or swirl pattern described on the cover, suggesting the volume was integrated into its professional binding shortly after the emblem was pasted.

<summary>This page serves as the frontispiece, featuring a Rosicrucian emblem of a pelican and a Rose Cross, titled "Hermetic Philosophy." It establishes the manuscript's focus on esoteric spiritual alchemy and divine enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic Philosophy, alchemy, Rosicrucianism, Pelican in her piety, Rose Cross, frontispiece, symbolism, esoteric tradition</keywords>

### Presented for the Benefit of States through Doubts

->against the "true wisdom" of the so-called genuine Freemasons or Golden Rosicrucians of the Old System<-

->by<-

## Magister Pianco,
<note>original: "Magister Pianco." This is the pseudonym of Hans Heinrich, Baron von Ecker und Eckhoffen (1750–1790). Although he was a founder of mystical orders, in this specific 1781 publication, he took a critical or "whistleblower" stance against the contemporary Rosicrucian systems.</note>

->ally of many circles.<-
<note>original: "vieler Kreisen Bundsverwandten." This indicates that the author was a member or "brother" of various <term>secret societies: private organizations, common in the 18th century, that used rituals and degrees to teach moral, mystical, or alchemical secrets</term>.</note>

---

->Amsterdam 1781.<-
<note>While the title page claims Amsterdam, many such esoteric or controversial books were actually printed in German cities like Berlin or Leipzig to evade local censors, using a false "Amsterdam" imprint to suggest a more liberal origin.</note>

# The Rosicrucian Exposed.

### Presented for the Benefit of States through Doubts

->against the "true wisdom" of the so-called genuine Freemasons or Golden Rosicrucians of the Old System<-
<note>The <term>Golden and Rosy Cross: an 18th-century German quasi-masonic order</term> was known for its focus on alchemy and strict hierarchy. The "Old System" refers to their claim of possessing ancient, uncorrupted knowledge.</note>

->by<-

## Magister Pianco,
<note>original: "Magister Pianco." This is the pseudonym of Hans Heinrich, Baron von Ecker und Eckhoffen (1750–1790). He was a significant figure in German esoteric circles who eventually became disillusioned and published several "whistleblower" texts exposing the secrets of the orders he once served.</note>

->To the<-

## Most Reverend Brother

# Phoebron
<note>Phoebron was the secret "Order name" (*nomen ordinis*) for Hans Carl von Ecker und Eckhoffen (1754–1809). He was a prominent figure in the "Gold- und Rosenkreuz" (Golden and Rosy Cross) during the 18th century. The author of this work was his own brother, who had become disillusioned with the society.</note>

### Head Director of the Upper Order
<note>The "Upper Order" refers to the administrative leadership and the highest degrees of the Rosicrucian hierarchy, which claimed to possess the most profound alchemical and spiritual secrets.</note>

<term>Rosicrucians: A secretive philosophical and fraternal society that emerged in the early 17th century. By the late 1700s, various "Rosicrucian" groups in Germany were heavily involved in alchemy, mysticism, and political maneuvering.</term>

### Highly Reverend, dear Brother Chief Director!
<note>The "Chief Director" refers to the head of the Rosicrucian Order, specifically Hans Carl von Ecker und Eckhoffen (Phoebron), who was the author’s brother.</note>

Although I do not have the honor of knowing you in person; you are nevertheless sufficiently known to me through your writings. They are, so to speak, treasures of knowledge <note>original: "Wißes" (Wissens)</note>, with which you do honor to nature and humanity: one marvels when one sees a man of your age enter upon the dangerous career of the <term>sophists</term> <note>In the 18th century, "sophist" was often used as a derogatory term for someone who engaged in overly complex, deceptive, or fallacious philosophical reasoning, particularly within esoteric circles.</note>, and sees him proceed courageously, with mighty strides. — To you, Highly Reverend Brother

Chief Director, this noble privilege was reserved before many hundreds.

I received the first reports of your diligence through Brother Hosmpina Neberus. <note>Like many names in Rosicrucian texts, "Hosmpina Neberus" is likely a pseudonym or an anagram for a specific contemporary figure within the order.</note> From time to time, he described to me your busy zeal and the untiring care which you apply uninterruptedly to the prosperity and growth of the high **Order of the Golden Rosicrucians**, and which would truly bring you great fame; if only your purpose were in accordance with the laws of nature and true honor. This is a wish that I make from my whole heart for you and your associates.

# Doubts
## against the true wisdom of the Rosicrucians.

### Chapter I.

**A short introduction to the secrets of the ancient sages, their properties and types.**

->§. 1.<-

There have been certain customs among nations at all times, which in part caused more and in part less sensation, and which often in themselves were neither good nor evil, but sometimes were of the most primary indifference. <note>The author suggests that many cultural traditions are fundamentally neutral or trivial before they are assigned deeper meaning.</note> There were, however, also customs that had reached the highest degree of importance, and which had the deepest influences on the

<note>The vocabulary list below appears to be a study aid or index of key terms used in this section.</note>
<vocab>Doubts, Wisdom, Rosicrucians, Secrets, Customs, Peoples</vocab>

<meta>catchword: Wohl (Welfare/Well-being). This word likely completes the final sentence: "which had the deepest influences on the welfare..."</meta>

<summary>This page begins Chapter I of the treatise, introducing the concept that while many historical customs are trivial, others possess profound significance and influence over the development of nations.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucians, ancient sages, mysteries, national customs, esoteric philosophy, Magister Pianco</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 15, completing the sentence regarding the impact of certain customs on the collective welfare of people.</meta>

well-being of entire nations. Such were often the individual and general treaties; such were the domestic institutions of the ancients; and so there were also alliances between individuals, and between several lineages <note>The German word *Geschlechtern* here refers to families, clans, or ancestral lines.</note>; and finally, there were alliances between the members of entire nations, which were linked with the most important secrets, and which very often had the most decisive influence on both religion and the state; secrets whose innermost workings <note>original: "innerste Beschäftigung" – this refers to the internal activities, rituals, or core business of a secret society.</note> remained hidden in eternal darkness through the strictest care of those who oversaw them.

§. 2. Thus, at various times, there were different types of secrets that had their true source in the deepest antiquity.

§. 3. These secrets were, in part, shared by both men and women—

## Chapter II.

**A further introduction to the mysteries of the ancient sages, known by the names of the world-wise <note>original: "Weltweisen"; a historical term for philosophers or those who studied the natural and metaphysical laws of the universe.</note> and Magi <term>Magi: originally a priestly caste of ancient Persia (Zoroastrianism) renowned for their knowledge of astronomy, alchemy, and philosophy; the term later became a general label for ancient seekers of hidden wisdom.</term>, and regarding their occupations.**

§. 1. Since the religious mysteries concerned the first part of the initiates and truly constituted the primary part of the secrets—those which exerted the most significant influence and held the greatest prestige within the system as a whole—the other kind of mysteries was dedicated to the wisdom of all things.

§. 2. This second covenant <note>original: "Bund"; here referring to a structured society or secret order.</note> stood in a precise connection with the first. However, both covenants—or both kinds of mysteries—shared the high priest and the supreme overseer in common; he held oversight over both simultaneously; thus the first members were also always the same, and always also community-

<summary>This page introduces the distinction between the religious mysteries and a second branch of hidden knowledge dedicated to universal wisdom, noting that both were led by the same high priest and elite members.</summary>
<keywords>Magi, World-wise, philosophers, religious mysteries, high priest, covenants, ancient sages, initiates</keywords>

communal; they were, so to speak, one in two, and two in one within an everlasting cause.

§. 3. Whoever wished to be accepted into the mysteries, and subsequently initiated, had to be a man of upright character and genuine intellectual strength; he also had to already possess great erudition: for they only admitted men from whom they could hold great hope that they would, in time, provide active benefit to the sacred covenant <note>original: "heiligen Bunde"; referring to the secret organization or society as a sacred brotherhood.</note>.

§. 4. For this very reason, however, they proceeded with the utmost caution in the selection of the initiates. To achieve this end, the supreme overseer of the covenant had chosen associates from his own midst, whose primary concern was to seek out the most useful members among the profane <term>the profane: from the Latin "pro fano" (outside the temple); a historical term used by secret societies to refer to the general, uninitiated public.</term> who might be admitted into the covenant.

§. 5. §. 6. [who] never came across the Jordan. **Magic** <term>magic</term> <note>original: "Magie"</note> and **history** <term>history</term> <note>original: "Geschichte"</note>, and **chronology** <term>chronology</term> <note>original: "Zeitrechnung"; the science of determining the sequence and dates of past events.</note> are entirely different things.

§. 20. §. 21. All those sciences that could be counted among the primary sciences of the ancient **Magi** <term>Magi (German: Magen): Ancient Persian priest-sages often associated in this era with hidden natural philosophy and political influence</term> and the knowledge of natural things, began to involve themselves in interventions into the constitutions of political states—actions that the **Knights Templar** never dared, and could never have dared. The **Knights Templar** were wretched political amateurs, whereas the **Jesuits**, by contrast, had exhausted **Machiavelli’s** <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the Italian diplomat whose name became synonymous with the pragmatic, and often ruthless, pursuit of political power</note> art and science, and had even noticeably improved upon it.

<summary>This page concludes the history of the Jesuit Order's suppression in 1773 and contrasts their political sophistication with the failure of smaller societies and the relative amateurism of the Knights Templar.</summary>

### Section 2.

Since not all <term>lodges</term> <note>The local organizational units where Freemasons meet and conduct their rituals.</note> entered into the great union together, the old system of the <term>Freemasons</term> <note>A fraternal organization that grew in popularity across Europe during the 18th-century Enlightenment, often centered on moral improvement and networking.</note> was sought out by many, adopted, and practiced in a formal manner.

### Section 3.

The old system of the Freemasons was very diverse—diverse in the <term>degrees</term> <note>The progressive levels of initiation and secret knowledge within the fraternity, typically starting with Apprentice, Fellowcraft, and Master Mason.</note> they conferred, diverse in their application, in their morality, and even in their allegory and as a whole; from such a <term>precarious</term> <note>original: "kritiſchen," here meaning a system that was unstable or highly contested.</note> so-called system, many subordinate ones—almost as precarious and sometimes just as incomprehensible—naturally had to arise; and it happened just as one had suspected.

### Section 4.

The <term>Grand Lodge</term> <note>original: "Hauptloge," referring to the central administrative body in Berlin, likely the Mother Lodge of the Three Globes.</note> in Berlin finally announced the true fundamental system, and there the matter rested. Every lodge and every union wanted to keep its special privileges, and since not all lodges could become completely unified, each one remained in that same original relationship.

<meta>catchword: §. 5.</meta>

§. 5. The first three <term>degrees</term> <note>The basic levels of initiation: Apprentice, Fellowcraft, and Master Mason.</note> of the Freemasons were accepted as universally the same, since they have been the true foundation of the society from time immemorial.

§. 6. Since the first three degrees were established, the various types of Brother Masons then decided upon the further arrangements according to which they would conduct their <term>work</term> <note>A term used by Masons to describe their ritual performances and moral self-improvement.</note>, and the formal clothing they wished to wear while doing so.

§. 7. This is all that was generally necessary to say in brief regarding a society that deserves the most sincere respect of the true, honest world.

They call it only the beginning of the <term>Probationary Year</term> <note>original: "Probejahr"; a period of testing to ensure a candidate is worthy of higher secrets.</note>, in order to test the <term>Initiate</term> whether he might be well-suited for higher things or not. There is no talk here of the French hybrid <term>Rosicrucian</term> <note>original: "Rose-Croix"; likely referring to a specific degree in French Freemasonry which the author views as an inferior or "mongrel" version of the true order.</note>. It deserves more pity than blame.

### §. 6. The first question that can be rightfully put to the Rosicrucians is prompted by the Table.

->a) First Doubt.<-

Is your Table correct? From where did you obtain it? Is it grounded in the initiations into the mysteries of the sages? What did these sages teach?

## Second Reminder.

One must wisely make a distinction between the Sages—who worshipped the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>original: "Demiurgos"; the creator-god or "craftsman" of the physical world.</note>, or the only true God, partly under the form of an equilateral triangle and partly under the form of the sun—and the Rosicrucians. The <term>hieroglyphs</term> <note>Here referring to the symbolic emblems and sacred signs used in ancient mystery schools.</note> of the Sages had no other object than to know religion, nature, and all associated properties; such was the nature of their entire teaching and its application. These Sages investigated nature and strove to know all its manifestations; yet, one does not find the slightest trace that they taught their members <term>alchemical</term> <note>original: "alchymistische"; relating to the transmutation of matter, which the author argues was not part of the original ancient wisdom.</note> tenets.

## Second Doubt.

c) This is the principal <term>cabalistic</term> <note>original: "kabalistische"; referring here to the "Christian Kabbalah," an early modern tradition that adapted Jewish mystical concepts for Christian alchemy and philosophy.</note> number.

You calculate from 1 to 9 and from 9 to 1, and you say: 1 is the beginning of all things. 3 signifies the **Trinity**, from which 4 active properties have proceeded, from which a 5th essence has emerged, through which wisdom has revealed itself in 7, and 9 is the end of all natural things.

Would you not be so kind as to tell me: what actually are <term>Kabbalah</term> and <term>Astrology</term>? Is it a science that rests on systematic grounds? Does it have axioms and fundamental principles? And from where does it derive these? — You know what the Kabbalah of the ancients was: Christians should make no use of a science that the <term>Savior</term> <note>original: "Seeligmacher"; literally "One who makes blessed," a common 18th-century German term for Jesus Christ.</note> himself regards as a deception. ## Fourth Doubt.

e) 5856. The number of the brotherhood, and the time when they all become **army commanders**. <note>The number 5856 likely refers to a year in a Masonic or mystical calendar; many societies added 4,000 years to the current date (Anno Lucis). "Army commanders" (Heerführer) suggests a militant or leadership-oriented grade within the secret hierarchy.</note>

May I ask you to explain thoroughly how you understand this concluding speech? ### First Reminder.

Whether it be that you give **Haschamaym** <note>Hebrew: *Ha-Shamayim*, meaning "The Heavens."</note>, or **Æsch-umayem** <note>A composite term likely intended by the Order to mean "Fire-Heavens," combining *Esh* (fire) and *Shamayim* (heavens).</note> to your Juniors as a <term>word of recognition</term> <note>A password or secret sign used to identify members of a specific rank or degree within a fraternal order like the Rosicrucians or Freemasons.</note>; tell me, what do you wish to express by it? ### Fourth Admonition.

**Zebaoth** <term>from the Hebrew *Sabaoth*, meaning "of Hosts"</term> is the word of the **Magister** <term>Master; a high-ranking officer or degree in the Rosicrucian hierarchy</term>. It signifies the **Infinite One**, and you represent Him under the figure of a **circle**. This circle, you teach, signifies your **Covenant** <note>The original German *Bund* refers to the secret society or Order itself</note>, which forms an infinite circle upon our globe; which contains within itself an incomprehensible power and incomparable beauty. 

Would you care to tell me: in what does the incomprehensible power and the incomparable beauty consist, of which you boast? Does the word **Power** have a secure connection with the criminal number **5856** <note>This likely refers to a year in the Masonic/Rosicrucian calendar (Anno Lucis or similar), where 5856 might correspond to 1856 or 1756 depending on the epoch used; the author calls it "criminal," likely implying it is a fraudulent or inflated historical claim</note>, or with the number where you all wish to be **commanders** <term>Heerführer; literally "army leaders," a play on the name Zebaoth/Lord of Hosts</term>? ### Fifth Admonition.

The word of the **Magi** <term>Magen; the plural of Magus, referring here to members of the high "Magus" degree of the Order</term> is **Jehovah** <term>The Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God in Hebrew</term>; and you represent it under the figure of an **equilateral triangle**. What do you intend to say by this? 

<margin>7</margin> Is it the triangle of the **Demiurge** <term>From the Greek *demiourgos*, meaning "craftsman." In various esoteric systems, this is the entity responsible for creating the physical world, sometimes distinguished from the supreme, purely spiritual God</term>? I should hope not. Here, my friends, lay the cross far from you <note>Likely a command to set aside their Christian pretenses or the physical insignia of the Order</note>. I will tell you who you are: a **Magus** or **Master** among you is a man who possesses all natural and supernatural sciences that a human being can possibly grasp. 

He can, so you say, cast down his staff, and it will become a serpent whenever he wishes—he can, through the power of magic, make withered trees full of the most beautiful fruits in the very moment he desires—he can obstruct the natural course of rivers—he can change the nature of the greatest rivers themselves—they must, you say, either dry up entirely—or [become] a black <meta>catchword: black</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the 'Magus' degree, questioning their use of the equilateral triangle and the name Jehovah, while mocking the supernatural powers—such as turning staffs into snakes or altering rivers—that the Order claims its masters possess.</summary>

From the kabbalistic names, the <term>Magi</term> <note>original: "Magi." ### Fourth Recollection.

You know that the newly admitted Brothers know no one among you except for the Superior who received them. He, and not you, is the one who presents the Table <term>Tabelle</term> to the **Profanes**, who portrays the most cunning fraud as infallible truth; because, for the most part, he unfortunately believes it himself to be the holiest truth. You know even more: that the **Labors** <term>Arbeiten</term> <note>In the context of Rosicrucianism and alchemy, "Labors" or "Works" refer to the specific chemical or spiritual experiments assigned to members to prove their progress.</note> which you communicate to the Brothers in the various degrees are false and without effect—excepting some secret natural processes and certainly many significant pieces where I grant you the honor of the science, because it belongs to you. <meta>This page transitions from the specific "Doubts" raised in the previous chapters to a formal logical "Closing Argument." The author employs a classic syllogism (Major Premise, Minor Premise, and an implied Conclusion) to dismantle the Rosicrucians' claims to ancient, supernatural knowledge.</meta>

### I.
# Closing Argument
## Major Premise <note>original: "Obersatz"; in formal logic, this is the first part of a syllogism, establishing a general rule.</note>

If the sciences <term>sciences</term> <note>original: "Wissenschaften"; referring here to the "branches of knowledge" or secret doctrines claimed by the Order.</note>, and the teachings associated with them—which you yourselves have established in your table as perfect, unalterable truths without the slightest reservation <note>original: "Klausel"; a legalistic term meaning a restrictive clause or "fine print."</note>—are such errors that the ancient so-called Magi <term>Magi</term> <note>original: "Magen"; the plural of Magus, referring to the legendary wise men or practitioners of secret arts in antiquity.</note> and Sages never thought of them, and if they were never grounded in the system of natural and—as you are pleased to say—so-called supernatural things, then your sciences are reprehensible, contrary to sound reason, fraudulent, and villainous sciences.

## Minor Premise <note>original: "Mittelsatz"; the second part of a syllogism that applies the general rule to a specific case.</note>

Now, your sciences, as you practice them, were never [practiced] by the ancient
<meta>Catchword: Magi</meta>

<summary>The author begins a formal logical refutation of the Rosicrucian Order, arguing that if their "sciences" contradict sound reason and the wisdom of the ancient Magi, they must be fraudulent. He begins the minor premise by stating that the ancients did not, in fact, practice the Order's version of these sciences.</summary>

6.) Your table and your entire teaching is physically false; and it is impossible that your *Masters* <note>original: "Magistri"</note> understand the two main sciences.

### Proof.

[Theoretics] <note>This text continues the footnote from the previous page regarding the "Junior" and "Theoretic" ranks of the Rosicrucian Order.</note> they recommend the *Golden Chain of Homer* <note>original: "Auream catenam Homeri." further there is no cause why the work of the **natural fire** should be hindered or interrupted. Now tell me: how can you produce the **natural fire** and the **effects of the natural fire**, when one runs the risk with your so-called **secret furnace** of setting half the city on fire? For you must know that I myself have helped to extinguish [the flames] of your **secret fire** with great difficulty. Tell me, how might it be possible to give the *artificial fire* <term>artificial fire</term> <note>original: *artificiellen Feuer*; the external heat provided by the alchemist’s furnace (athanor), which the author argues is a clumsy imitation of the "natural fire" found in the earth or within the matter itself.</note> the degree of the **natural fire**? That is: so that this fire produces within itself the efficacy and the activity of **working nature**, and maintains it until its ripeness—a fire that itself, like the **fire of the sun**, is inevitably subject to infinitely many accidents and changes? Tell me, how do you intend to force and bind **nature** in her quite free and **unrestricted workings**, which you do not know and, as you yourself know, cannot possibly know? 7.) **Your table and your science are metaphysically false.**

## Proof.

You say that your <term>Magi</term> <note>Latin plural of *magus*; here referring to the "wise men" or masters of the secret arts whom the alchemists claim to follow.</note> possess all natural and supernatural sciences. I well know which sciences are natural, and it is certain that man can learn and possess all natural sciences. In this, I am of one mind with you. But now tell me: which sciences are supernatural? And from whence do you derive these supernatural sciences? And which supernatural sciences can man comprehend? To point c) You say further: that the entire order of the **world-system** <note>original: *Weltgebäudes*; a common 18th-century term for the structure of the universe, often compared to a giant building or a clockwork machine.</note> stands at your command, etc., and more such shameless things. <page-num>184</page-num>

### 8. All Magi of all times were either **skilled performers** <term>skilled performers: *Künstler*; in this context, the author refers not to fine artists, but to "con-artists" or "illusionists" who use manual dexterity and stagecraft to simulate supernatural effects.</term> or **scoundrels**.

->Proof.<-

That the Magi of Egypt were fraudsters has long been proven by all scholars, and even **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist. In Book 30 of his *Natural History*, he provides a scathing history of magic, characterizing it as a fraudulent blend of medicine, religion, and astrology.</note> gives this testimony—which is so "honorable" for you—regarding the Magi whom **Nero** <note>The Roman Emperor Nero (r. 54–68 AD) was notoriously fascinated by the occult, but according to Pliny, his experiments with the Magi of the East only proved the emptiness of their claims.</note> had brought from the East to Rome because he wished to command even the gods.*) Such were the Magi of the— <note>The sentence ends mid-thought at the bottom of the page.</note>

*) Nero was, as I have shown (Chapter I, §. 10. see notes), rejected by the High Priest during the initiations into the **Eleusinian Mysteries** <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece. To be rejected from these was a sign of extreme moral infamy, as even "barbarians" were often admitted, but murderers and the impious were excluded.</note>. He was not accepted into the holy covenant. The Magi, however, did not deny him entry into the **secrets of nature** <note>original: *Geheimnissen der Natur*; a term used by alchemists and occultists to describe the hidden physical and spiritual laws they claimed to manipulate.</note>. To that end, he had them brought from the East and showered them with gifts. He spared no expense, providing even the rarest and most noble animals...

<summary>The author begins a historical critique of magic, citing Pliny the Elder’s account of Nero’s failed attempts to master the occult to prove that "Magi" are merely tricksters who exploit the curiosity of the powerful.</summary>

...conditioned in older times. Of the Magi whom you claim as belonging to your covenant, one of the most prominent is **Albertus Magnus**. **Albert**\*) was a famous man and a pious Bishop in **Regensburg**,

<note>The following section continues the long footnote from the previous page regarding Nero’s experiments with the Magi.</note>

...even humans were sought out and slaughtered for their sacrifices and rituals. He [Nero], however, was too cunning and did not allow himself to be blinded by their deceptions; he discovered their follies and wished to have nothing more to do with them. "**First**," says **Pliny** in **Natural History, Book 30, Chapter 2**, "he [Nero] desired to command the gods, and nothing could be more ambitious. No one ever favored any of the arts more strongly. For this, neither wealth, nor strength, nor the talent for learning were lacking. — It is a vast and indubitable example of a false art, which Nero eventually abandoned — Therefore, let it be a settled conviction that magic is detestable, useless, and empty; yet it possesses certain shadows of truth, though in these it is the arts of poisoning <term>arts of poisoning: *veneficas artes*; in Latin, this term refers to sorcery involving drugs, potions, or poisons, which Pliny distinguishes from the supposedly "higher" magic the practitioners claimed to possess.</term> that prevail, not the magical ones..."

<note>original Latin: "Primum... imperare Diis concupivit, nec quicquam generosius voluit. Nemo unquam ulli artium validius favit. ### b) Is one bound to fulfill the oath once sworn to you?

**Answer: No.**

§. 14. It has been proven to you in the meantime that you (§. 13.) do not have the slightest right to impose an **oath** upon your candidates <note>original: "Kandidaten." In the context of secret societies, these are initiates who have not yet reached the higher grades.</note>, and it follows quite naturally: that those who do not have the slightest right to undertake this or that action—even if they actually do so—are performing an unfair and highly unjust action. Consequently, those whom they have overwhelmed <note>original: "übertäubet," literally "deafened" or "stunned," implying the candidates were psychologically manipulated or dazzled into compliance.</note> into performing this act are likewise not bound to uphold the duration of this act; on the contrary, as soon as they recognize the injustice of it in its true sense, they must renounce it. I shall, however, present this question to you even more clearly in brief.

And specifically:

<vocab>Oath, Candidates, Right, Action, Injustice, Understanding, To impose an oath, To renounce</vocab>

a) One is not required to satisfy your oath; because it is:
1. against God,
2. against the Law of Nature <note>original: "Recht der Natur." This refers to the philosophical concept that certain rights and moral values are inherent in human nature and can be understood through reason.</note>, and
3. against the general Law of Nations <note>original: "Rechte aller Völker." Historically known as *jus gentium*, the legal principles common to all civilized nations.</note>.

## §. 15. Your oath is 1) against God.

### Proof.

When I confirm this or that matter with an oath, I bear witness through God in my soul: that I know, for example, this or that, in such a way and no other, or that I bind myself to this or that as certainly and as perfectly truly as God is God. Now, you present the table (Chapter VIII, §. 7) to your candidate <note>Initiates entering the society's lower degrees.</note> before he swears the oath to you; you assure him by the word of eternal truth: that you—as your brothers reach this or that degree according to the table—will [teach] them all the sciences indicated in the table sincerely and without reservation

<meta>tung</meta> <note>The syllable "tung" is the continuation of the word "Zurückhaltung" (reservation) carried over to the next page.</note>

**Oath:** a solemn appeal to God to witness the truth of a statement.
**God:** the ultimate authority used by the society to validate their claims.
**Law of Nature:** the inherent moral order.
**Law of Nations:** the shared legal standards of the world.
**Candidates:** those seeking entry into the Rosicrucian degrees.
**Table:** the curriculum or chart of degrees and secret sciences.
**Sciences:** the specific occult or alchemical knowledge promised by the order.
**Reservation:** the act of withholding information (which the order claims not to do).

<summary>The author argues that the Rosicrucian oath is invalid because it contradicts divine, natural, and international law. He begins a proof showing how the order uses God's name to falsely guarantee that they will teach candidates "secret sciences" without reservation.</summary>

# §. 17. **Your Oath** 3) is contrary to the universal rights of all peoples <note>The "rights of all peoples" (Völkerrechte) refers to the early modern concept of the Law of Nations, the precursor to international law.</note>.

Whatever is against God is against Nature; and whatever is against Nature is against the universal rights of peoples: for no law can <term>exclude (excipiren): From the Latin 'excipere,' meaning to exempt or make an exception for something.</term> nature, as all laws together have the foundation of their rights in the unchangeable and ever-constant <term>Natural Law (Naturrecht): The philosophical concept that certain rights and moral values are inherent in human nature and can be understood through reason.</term>. ### c) What is your oath?

->Answer: A godless, reprehensible act.<-

§. 18. It has been proven to you that your oath is unlawful and unauthorized (Chapter IX, §. 13), against God (§. 15), against the <term>Law of Nature (Recht der Natur): The concept that certain rights and moral values are inherent in human nature and can be understood through reason, independent of man-made laws.</term> (§. 16), and against all general <term>Rights of Nations (Rechte der Völker): The legal and ethical principles traditionally recognized as governing the interactions between different peoples or states.</term> (§. 17). §. 19. ### Decorative Endpaper

The final surface of this volume is covered in a traditional <term>marbled paper</term> <note>a decorative paper created by floating pigments on a liquid size and "combing" or "marbling" them into a pattern before transferring them to the sheet</note>. This specific design is known as a **stone** or **shell** marble pattern, characterized by its organic, cell-like appearance.

The pattern features large, rounded, and irregular cells in shades of light grey and off-white. These lighter "stones" are set against a deep, dark indigo or black matrix that acts as the background. Interspersed throughout this dark mortar are delicate, organic veins of yellow and red pigments, creating a variegated texture that was highly prized in historical bookbinding for its durability and aesthetic appeal.

<note>Marbled endpapers were not merely decorative; they also served a practical purpose. The complex patterns helped to hide the "turn-ins" (the edges of the leather or cloth covering the boards) and the structural bits of the binding, providing a clean and elegant finish to the book's interior.</note>

<summary>A decorative marbled endpaper in a stone or shell pattern featuring grey, indigo, red, and yellow pigments, marking the interior back cover of the manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>marbled paper, endpaper, bookbinding, stone marble pattern, pastedown, decorative arts</keywords>

### Decorative Endpaper

The final surface of this volume is covered in a traditional <term>marbled paper</term> <note>a decorative paper created by floating pigments on a liquid size—a thickening agent—and "combing" or "marbling" them into a pattern before transferring them to the sheet</note>. This specific design is known as a **stone** or **shell** marble pattern, characterized by its organic, cell-like appearance.

The pattern features large, rounded, and irregular cells in shades of light grey and off-white. These lighter "stones" are set against a deep, dark indigo or black matrix that acts as the background. Interspersed throughout this dark mortar are delicate, organic veins of ochre yellow and crimson red pigments, creating a variegated texture that was highly prized in historical bookbinding for its durability and aesthetic appeal.

Along the right edge, there is a visible transition to a different marbling style, likely where the paper meets the inner "turn-in" <note>the part of the leather or cloth cover folded over the edge of the cardboard binder's board</note>. This section displays a combed or wavy pattern, introducing additional streaks of red, green, and yellow.

<note>Marbled endpapers were not merely decorative; they also served a practical purpose. The complex patterns helped to hide the "turn-ins" and the structural bits of the binding, providing a clean and elegant finish to the book's interior.</note>

<summary>A decorative marbled endpaper in a stone or shell pattern featuring grey, indigo, red, and yellow pigments, marking the interior back cover of the manuscript.</summary>

### Front Cover

The exterior of this manuscript is presented in a classic <term>half-binding</term> <note>a style of bookbinding where the spine and corners are covered in a different, usually more durable material than the sides</note>. 

The central panel is covered in **marbled paper** featuring a complex "combed" or "swirled" pattern. This decorative technique creates fluid, interlocking shapes in a palette of deep red, dark green, and tan. 

The spine and the corners are reinforced with a dark green, textured material—likely leather or a heavy cloth like buckram. This combination of materials was a common choice for both durability and aesthetic appeal. Small signs of age, such as slight wear along the corners and the edges of the spine, reflect the history of the volume's use and storage.

<summary>The front cover of the manuscript, featuring a dark green half-binding and a decorative combed marbled paper in red, green, and tan.</summary>
<keywords>binding, marbled paper, half-bound, manuscript cover, bookbinding</keywords>
